<a id='question-025'></a>

### Question 25: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 01-25 (or nitroglycerin(vasodilation).Electrocardiogramsh)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and histological features of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM), specifically focusing on the molecular basis of sarcomere dysfunction and its impact on myocardial structure and function. (理解肥厚型心肌病的病理生理学和组织学特征，特别是关注肌节缺陷的分子基础及其对心肌结构和功能的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of exertional dyspnea, chest pain, and near-syncope during a recent basketball game. He has a family history of sudden cardiac death in his father at age 35 during exercise. Physical examination reveals a harsh systolic murmur that increases with Valsalva maneuver and decreases with squatting. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH) and deep, narrow Q waves in the inferior leads. Echocardiogram confirms asymmetric septal hypertrophy, left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction, and systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *MYH7* gene. Endomyocardial biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination shows marked interstitial fibrosis, myofiber disarray, and thickened coronary arteries.

Which of the following molecular abnormalities is MOST likely responsible for the sarcomere dysfunction observed in this patient?

(A) Mutation leading to decreased expression of α-actinin, resulting in weakened sarcomere structure.
(B) Mutation leading to increased expression of cardiac troponin T (cTnT), causing hypercontractility.
(C) Mutation leading to decreased expression of myosin heavy chain (β-MHC), resulting in impaired contractility.
(D) Mutation leading to increased expression of myosin heavy chain (β-MHC), causing hypercontractility and diastolic dysfunction.
(E) Mutation leading to decreased expression of titin, resulting in impaired passive elasticity.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male with exertional symptoms (dyspnea, chest pain, near-syncope), family history of sudden cardiac death (SCD) during exercise. This points towards a genetic cardiac condition, potentially HCM.
- **Physical Exam:** Harsh systolic murmur increasing with Valsalva (decreases preload, worsens LVOT obstruction) and decreasing with squatting (increases preload/afterload, reduces LVOT obstruction). This is characteristic of dynamic LVOT obstruction seen in HCM.
- **ECG:** LVH and deep, narrow Q waves (often seen in septal hypertrophy). Consistent with HCM.
- **Echocardiogram:** Asymmetric septal hypertrophy, LVOT obstruction, SAM of the mitral valve. These are hallmark features of obstructive HCM.
- **Genetic Testing:** *MYH7* gene mutation. *MYH7* encodes β-myosin heavy chain (β-MHC), a key component of the sarcomere. Mutations in *MYH7* are a common cause of HCM.
- **Endomyocardial Biopsy:** Interstitial fibrosis, myofiber disarray, thickened coronary arteries. Myofiber disarray is a characteristic histological finding in HCM.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular abnormality responsible for sarcomere dysfunction. Given the clinical picture of HCM and the *MYH7* mutation, the question focuses on the functional consequence of this mutation on the sarcomere.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **HCM Pathophysiology:** HCM is primarily a disease of the sarcomere, characterized by myocardial hypertrophy (often asymmetric, involving the septum), myofiber disarray, interstitial fibrosis, and small vessel disease. The underlying cause is often a genetic mutation affecting sarcomeric proteins.
- **Sarcomere Function:** The sarcomere is the basic contractile unit of muscle. Key proteins include actin, myosin (α-actinin, β-MHC, cTnT, cTnI, cTnC), troponin, tropomyosin, titin, and nebulin. Proper function requires coordinated interaction of these proteins.
- ***MYH7* Gene and β-MHC:** The *MYH7* gene encodes the β-myosin heavy chain (β-MHC), which forms the thick filament of the sarcomere. Mutations in *MYH7* are the most common genetic cause of HCM (~40% of cases).
- **Molecular Consequence of *MYH7* Mutations:** Most HCM-associated *MYH7* mutations lead to *increased* contractility (hypercontractility) of the sarcomere. This is often due to mutations that enhance the interaction between myosin and actin, or alter the ATPase activity of myosin. This hypercontractility, combined with abnormal calcium handling and diastolic dysfunction, contributes to the hypertrophy, fibrosis, and outflow obstruction seen in HCM. The increased force generation leads to increased wall stress and subsequent hypertrophy.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) Decreased α-actinin: α-actinin is involved in anchoring actin filaments. Decreased expression would likely weaken the sarcomere, not cause the hypercontractility seen in HCM.
    - (B) Increased cTnT: While cTnT is part of the thin filament and regulates contraction, mutations in *MYH7* directly affect the thick filament (β-MHC). Increased cTnT expression is not the primary mechanism linked to *MYH7* mutations in HCM.
    - (C) Decreased β-MHC: Decreased β-MHC would impair contractility, which is opposite to the hypercontractility typically associated with HCM-causing *MYH7* mutations.
    - (D) Increased β-MHC: Increased β-MHC expression or altered function due to *MYH7* mutations leads to hypercontractility. This hypercontractility contributes to the hypertrophy and diastolic dysfunction characteristic of HCM. This aligns with the known pathophysiology of HCM caused by *MYH7* mutations.
    - (E) Decreased titin: Titin provides passive elasticity to the sarcomere. Decreased titin would impair passive recoil, potentially contributing to diastolic dysfunction, but the primary defect in HCM related to *MYH7* mutations is hypercontractility due to altered β-MHC function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. Mutations in sarcomeric proteins in HCM typically cause *increased* contractility or impaired relaxation, not weakened structure due to decreased α-actinin. α-actinin is crucial for sarcomere integrity.
- (B): Incorrect. While troponin T is involved in calcium regulation and contraction, the specific mutation identified is in *MYH7* (β-MHC). The primary effect of *MYH7* mutations in HCM is related to the thick filament's contractile properties, leading to hypercontractility, not primarily mediated by increased cTnT expression.
- (C): Incorrect. HCM is characterized by *hyper*contractility, often due to mutations that enhance myosin function. Decreased β-MHC would lead to *hypo*contractility, which is inconsistent with the pathophysiology of HCM.
- (E): Incorrect. Titin mutations can cause certain cardiomyopathies (e.g., dilated cardiomyopathy, restrictive cardiomyopathy), affecting passive stiffness. While diastolic dysfunction is prominent in HCM, the primary molecular defect linked to the *MYH7* mutation is hypercontractility, not primarily impaired passive elasticity due to decreased titin.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM) | Dilated Cardiomyopathy (DCM) | Restrictive Cardiomyopathy (RCM) |
|----------------------|------------------------------------|------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**   | Sarcomere dysfunction (often hypercontractility) | Myocyte damage/dysfunction | Impaired diastolic filling (stiff ventricles) |
| **Wall Thickness**   | Increased (often asymmetric)        | Normal or Increased          | Normal                            |
| **Ventricular Size** | Normal or Decreased                | Increased                    | Normal                            |
| **Systolic Function**| Often Normal or Hyperdynamic       | Decreased                    | Normal or Decreased                |
| **Diastolic Function**| Impaired                           | Impaired                     | Impaired                           |
| **Common Gene**      | *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*                   | *TTN*, *LMNA*                 | *MYH7*, *TNNI3*, *TNNT2*, *TRPM6* |
| **Histology**        | Myofiber disarray, fibrosis        | Myocyte hypertrophy, fibrosis| Fibrosis, infiltrates              |
| **Molecular Effect** | Increased β-MHC contractility     | Impaired sarcomere function | Impaired relaxation/filling        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
HCM is primarily a genetic disorder affecting sarcomeric proteins, most commonly *MYH7* (β-MHC) and *MYBPC3* (myosin-binding protein C). Mutations often lead to hypercontractility, myofiber disarray, and diastolic dysfunction. Key differentials include DCM (impaired systolic function, dilated ventricles) and RCM (impaired diastolic filling due to stiff ventricles). Understanding the specific molecular defect (e.g., increased β-MHC contractility in HCM) is crucial for understanding the pathophysiology.

<a id='question-026'></a>

### Question 26: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 01-26 (depression (subendocardialischemia).)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the ECG changes associated with subendocardial ischemia, specifically ST-segment depression, and differentiating it from other causes of ST depression or other ECG abnormalities. (理解心肌缺血，特别是心内膜缺血，相关的ECG变化，特别是ST段压低，并将其与其他ST段压低原因或其他ECG异常区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department complaining of substernal chest pain that started 30 minutes ago while mowing the lawn. The pain is described as a pressure sensation radiating to his left arm. His vital signs are: BP 145/90 mmHg, HR 95 bpm, RR 18 breaths/min, SpO2 98% on room air. Physical examination reveals a diaphoretic patient with no murmurs, rubs, or gallops. An initial 12-lead ECG is obtained. The ECG shows sinus rhythm at 95 bpm, with ST-segment depression in leads V4-V6 and T-wave inversion in leads II, III, and aVF. Cardiac troponin I is elevated at 1.2 ng/mL (reference range <0.04 ng/mL).

Which of the following pathophysiological mechanisms BEST explains the ST-segment depression observed in this patient's ECG?

(A) Epicardial ischemia leading to transmural injury.
(B) Subendocardial ischemia leading to impaired repolarization.
(C) Pericarditis causing diffuse ST elevation and PR depression.
(D) Ventricular hypertrophy causing ST depression and T-wave inversion in limb leads.
(E) Bundle branch block causing wide QRS complexes and secondary ST-T changes.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia) presenting with classic symptoms of acute coronary syndrome (ACS) - substernal chest pain radiating to the left arm, onset during exertion.
- **Vital Signs:** Elevated BP and HR are consistent with pain and sympathetic activation during ACS.
- **Physical Exam:** Diaphoresis is common in ACS. Absence of murmurs/rubs/gallops makes valvular disease, pericarditis, or heart failure less likely as the primary cause of the chest pain.
- **ECG Findings:** Sinus rhythm, ST-segment depression in V4-V6 (anterolateral leads), and T-wave inversion in II, III, aVF (inferior leads). This pattern is highly suggestive of subendocardial ischemia. ST depression typically indicates myocardial ischemia, while ST elevation indicates transmural injury/infarction. The specific leads involved (V4-V6, II, III, aVF) suggest involvement of the anterolateral and inferior walls, often related to left anterior descending (LAD) or right coronary artery (RCA) occlusion.
- **Cardiac Troponin:** Elevated troponin confirms myocardial injury (necrosis), indicating an NSTEMI (Non-ST Elevation Myocardial Infarction) in this context, given the ST depression.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the ST-segment depression.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Subendocardial Ischemia:** Occurs when blood flow to the inner layer of the heart muscle (subendocardium) is reduced, typically due to coronary artery stenosis or occlusion. The subendocardium is the most vulnerable region to ischemia because it receives blood supply primarily during diastole when the coronary arteries are compressed by the contracting myocardium.
- **ECG Changes in Subendocardial Ischemia:** Ischemia disrupts normal myocardial electrical activity. Specifically, it affects repolarization. During ischemia, the action potential duration shortens, and the recovery of the resting membrane potential is accelerated. This leads to a faster repolarization in the ischemic areas compared to the normally perfused areas.
- **ST-Segment Depression:** The ST segment on the ECG represents the period between ventricular depolarization (QRS complex) and repolarization (T wave). ST depression occurs because the accelerated repolarization in the ischemic subendocardium causes the end of the QRS complex to be at the same electrical potential as the beginning of the repolarization phase in the normally perfused myocardium. This creates a downward deflection (depression) relative to the baseline isoelectric line.
- **T-Wave Inversion:** Ischemia can also cause T-wave inversion, reflecting abnormal repolarization currents.
- **Contrast with Transmural Ischemia:** Transmural ischemia involves the full thickness of the myocardial wall (epicardium to endocardium). This typically causes ST-segment elevation because the entire wall is injured and depolarized, leading to a delayed repolarization and a positive deflection (elevation) during the repolarization phase.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Epicardial ischemia leading to transmural injury: Epicardial ischemia, if severe enough to cause transmural injury, typically results in ST-segment *elevation*, not depression. ST elevation indicates full-thickness myocardial injury. This is incorrect.
- (C) Pericarditis causing diffuse ST elevation and PR depression: Pericarditis causes inflammation of the pericardium. The characteristic ECG finding is diffuse ST-segment *elevation* (often concave upwards) and PR-segment depression, not ST depression. This is incorrect.
- (D) Ventricular hypertrophy causing ST depression and T-wave inversion in limb leads: Left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH) can cause ST depression and T-wave inversion, but typically in the *limb leads* (I, II, III, aVL, aVF, V5, V6) due to abnormal ventricular depolarization. While this patient has T-wave inversions in inferior limb leads, the ST depression is primarily in the precordial leads (V4-V6), which is more characteristic of ischemia. Furthermore, LVH doesn't explain the elevated troponin. This is incorrect.
- (E) Bundle branch block causing wide QRS complexes and secondary ST-T changes: Bundle branch blocks (BBB) cause widening of the QRS complex. Secondary ST-T changes can occur (e.g., discordant ST elevation or depression relative to the QRS complex), but the primary finding is the wide QRS. This patient's ECG shows a normal QRS duration (implied by "sinus rhythm at 95 bpm" without mention of QRS widening), and the ST depression is the most prominent finding related to the acute event. This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Subendocardial Ischemia (NSTEMI) | Transmural Ischemia (STEMI) | Pericarditis          | LVH                     | Bundle Branch Block |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|-------------------------------|-----------------------|-------------------------|----------------------|
| **Primary ECG Change** | ST Depression, T-wave Inversion   | ST Elevation                 | Diffuse ST Elevation, PR Depression | ST Depression (limb leads), T-wave Inversion (limb leads) | Wide QRS Complex     |
| **Mechanism**        | Impaired Repolarization (faster)  | Delayed Repolarization        | Pericardial Inflammation | Abnormal Depolarization | Conduction Delay    |
| **Myocardial Injury** | Yes (Subendocardium)              | Yes (Transmural)              | No                    | No                      | No                   |
| **Troponin**         | Elevated                          | Elevated                      | Normal                | Normal                  | Normal               |
| **Typical Leads**    | V4-V6, II, III, aVF (in this case) | Depends on artery occlusion  | All leads (diffuse)   | I, II, III, aVL, aVF, V5, V6 | N/A                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
ST-segment depression on ECG typically indicates myocardial ischemia, most commonly subendocardial ischemia. This occurs due to accelerated repolarization in the ischemic zone. Differentiating ST depression due to ischemia from other causes like LVH or BBB is crucial. ST elevation indicates transmural injury. Key differentials for ST depression include subendocardial ischemia, LVH, BBB, digoxin effect, and hypokalemia. Always correlate ECG findings with clinical context and biomarkers like troponin.

<a id='question-027'></a>

### Question 027: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 01-27 (• Prinzmetal variant angina is caused by coronary )
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of Prinzmetal angina (variant angina), specifically the role of coronary artery spasm and its underlying mechanism involving endothelial dysfunction and smooth muscle hyperreactivity. (理解 Prinzmetal 心绞痛的病理生理机制，特别是冠状动脉痉挛的作用以及其潜在的内皮功能障碍和血管平滑肌过度反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing chest pain that began at rest approximately 30 minutes ago. He denies any recent exertion or emotional stress preceding the pain. His past medical history is significant for hypertension and hyperlipidemia, both managed with lisinopril and atorvastatin, respectively. He is a current smoker (1 pack per day for 35 years). On examination, his vital signs are: BP 140/90 mmHg, HR 90 bpm, RR 18 breaths/min, SpO2 96% on room air. Physical examination is unremarkable except for mild anxiety. An initial ECG shows transient ST-segment elevation in leads V2-V4, which resolves spontaneously within 15 minutes. Cardiac biomarkers (troponin I) are negative. A coronary angiogram reveals no significant fixed stenoses (>70%) in the left anterior descending (LAD), circumflex, or right coronary arteries. However, during the angiogram, the patient experienced another episode of chest pain, and the angiogram showed transient, severe spasm of the proximal LAD artery, causing near-complete occlusion.

What is the most likely underlying mechanism causing this patient's symptoms?

(A) Rupture of an atherosclerotic plaque with subsequent thrombus formation leading to acute coronary occlusion.
(B) Increased myocardial oxygen demand due to severe hypertension and tachycardia, exceeding coronary blood flow supply.
(C) Transient coronary artery spasm due to endothelial dysfunction and smooth muscle hyperreactivity.
(D) Microvascular dysfunction characterized by impaired vasodilation of small coronary arterioles.
(E) Embolization of thrombus or plaque debris from a proximal coronary artery to a distal branch, causing acute ischemia.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male with risk factors for coronary artery disease (CAD) - hypertension, hyperlipidemia, smoking.
- **Symptoms:** Severe chest pain at rest, characteristic of unstable angina or myocardial infarction (MI).
- **ECG:** Transient ST-segment elevation in V2-V4, resolving spontaneously. This suggests acute myocardial ischemia, but the transient nature and resolution without biomarker elevation point away from a typical MI caused by fixed occlusion and necrosis.
- **Cardiac Biomarkers:** Negative troponin I rules out significant myocardial necrosis (MI).
- **Coronary Angiogram:** No significant fixed stenoses. This rules out stable angina or MI caused by a thrombotic occlusion of a pre-existing severe stenosis.
- **Provocative Angiogram:** Transient, severe spasm of the proximal LAD artery during chest pain episode, causing near-complete occlusion. This is the definitive finding.
- **Diagnosis:** The clinical presentation (chest pain at rest), ECG changes (transient ST elevation), negative biomarkers, absence of fixed stenosis on angiography, and demonstration of coronary artery spasm during angiography are classic features of Prinzmetal angina (variant angina).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Prinzmetal Angina Pathophysiology:** It is caused by transient, focal spasm of a coronary artery, leading to a sudden decrease in myocardial blood flow and ischemia.
- **Underlying Mechanism:** The spasm is primarily due to hyperreactivity of the coronary artery smooth muscle and endothelial dysfunction.
- **Endothelial Dysfunction:** Impaired production of vasodilators (like nitric oxide, NO) and/or increased production of vasoconstrictors (like endothelin-1). NO is crucial for maintaining vascular tone and preventing spasm. Reduced NO bioavailability leads to unopposed vasoconstriction.
- **Smooth Muscle Hyperreactivity:** Increased sensitivity of the smooth muscle cells to various stimuli (e.g., acetylcholine, serotonin, catecholamines, cold exposure, smoking). This hyperreactivity can be triggered by factors that normally cause mild vasoconstriction or have no effect.
- **Plaque Rupture (Distractor A):** While plaque rupture can cause spasm, it typically leads to thrombus formation and persistent occlusion, resulting in MI (positive biomarkers) or unstable angina with fixed stenosis. The angiogram showed no fixed stenosis and spasm was transient.
- **Increased Oxygen Demand (Distractor B):** While increased demand can cause angina, it typically occurs with exertion (stable angina) or severe stress. This patient's pain occurred at rest, and the angiogram showed spasm, not simply inadequate supply due to high demand.
- **Microvascular Dysfunction (Distractor D):** This involves dysfunction of the small coronary arterioles and capillaries, often seen in syndrome X. While it can cause angina, it doesn't typically manifest as large vessel spasm seen on angiography and transient ST elevation.
- **Embolization (Distractor E):** Embolization causes acute occlusion, usually leading to MI (positive biomarkers) and persistent ischemia, not transient spasm.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Rupture of an atherosclerotic plaque with subsequent thrombus formation...**: This describes the typical mechanism for acute myocardial infarction (MI) or unstable angina. The patient has negative troponins and no significant fixed stenosis on angiography, making this unlikely. While plaque rupture *can* trigger spasm, the primary event here, demonstrated on angiography, is the spasm itself, not a thrombotic occlusion secondary to rupture.
- (B): **Increased myocardial oxygen demand...**: This mechanism is more characteristic of stable angina (exertional) or unstable angina precipitated by stress/tachycardia. The patient's pain occurred at rest, and the angiogram showed spasm, not evidence of demand ischemia.
- (D): **Microvascular dysfunction...**: This refers to problems with the small coronary vessels, often associated with Syndrome X. It typically causes diffuse ischemia and may not produce the focal, severe spasm seen on angiography or the characteristic ST elevation pattern.
- (E): **Embolization of thrombus or plaque debris...**: This causes acute occlusion, leading to MI or severe ischemia. The angiogram showed transient spasm, not a persistent occlusion due to embolus, and the patient had negative biomarkers.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Prinzmetal Angina (Variant Angina) | Acute MI (Plaque Rupture/Thrombus) | Stable Angina (Fixed Stenosis) | Microvascular Dysfunction (Syndrome X) | Embolic Occlusion |
|----------------------|------------------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|----------------------------------------|-------------------|
| **Primary Cause**    | Coronary Artery Spasm              | Plaque Rupture + Thrombus        | Fixed Coronary Stenosis          | Small Vessel Dysfunction              | Embolus           |
| **Pain Onset**       | Often at rest, night               | Often exertion, rest, or sudden   | Typically with exertion         | Often atypical, rest, exertion         | Sudden            |
| **ECG**              | Transient ST Elevation             | Persistent ST Elevation/Changes   | ST Depression/T-wave inversion   | Often normal or non-specific          | ST Elevation/Changes |
| **Biomarkers**       | Negative                           | Positive (Troponin)               | Negative                         | Negative                               | Positive          |
| **Angiography**      | No fixed stenosis, spasm seen      | Fixed stenosis, thrombus          | Fixed stenosis (>70%)            | Normal epicardial arteries             | Occlusion        |
| **Underlying Mech.** | Endothelial dysfunction, Smooth muscle hyperreactivity | Thrombosis, Inflammation         | Atherosclerosis                   | Endothelial dysfunction, inflammation | Thrombus/Plaque   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prinzmetal angina is caused by coronary artery spasm, often occurring at rest, and is characterized by transient ST-segment elevation on ECG and negative cardiac biomarkers. Angiography typically shows no significant fixed stenosis but may demonstrate spasm during a symptomatic episode. Key differentials include acute MI (positive biomarkers, fixed occlusion/thrombus), stable angina (exertional pain, fixed stenosis), and microvascular dysfunction (Syndrome X). The hallmark is the transient spasm itself.

<a id='question-028'></a>

### Question 028: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 01-28 (produces episodic chest pain often at rest; it is )
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of vasospastic angina (Prinzmetal angina) and its distinction from other causes of chest pain, specifically focusing on the role of coronary artery smooth muscle contraction and the underlying cellular mechanisms. (理解血管痉挛性心绞痛（Prinzmetal心绞痛）的病理生理学，并将其与其他胸痛原因区分开来，特别是关注冠状动脉平滑肌收缩的作用以及潜在的细胞机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing chest pain that began approximately 30 minutes ago while he was sleeping. He denies any recent exertion or emotional stress preceding the pain. His past medical history is significant for hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and type 2 diabetes mellitus, all well-controlled with medication. He is a current smoker (1 pack per day for 30 years). On examination, his vital signs are stable: BP 130/70 mmHg, HR 75 bpm, RR 16/min, SpO2 98% on room air. Physical examination is unremarkable except for a slightly anxious demeanor. An initial ECG shows transient ST-segment elevation in leads V1-V4, which resolves spontaneously within 15 minutes. Cardiac troponin levels are within normal limits. A coronary angiogram performed later reveals no significant fixed stenoses but demonstrates diffuse, non-obstructive coronary artery disease. During the angiogram, provocative testing with intracoronary acetylcholine administration induces transient, severe spasm in the left anterior descending (LAD) artery, accompanied by recurrent chest pain and ST-segment elevation.

Which of the following mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's recurrent chest pain?

(A) Rupture of an atherosclerotic plaque leading to thrombus formation and coronary occlusion.
(B) Increased myocardial oxygen demand due to elevated heart rate and contractility during exertion.
(C) Transient endothelial dysfunction and excessive smooth muscle contraction in response to a stimulus.
(D) Progressive intimal hyperplasia and fibrosis causing fixed luminal narrowing of the coronary arteries.
(E) Microvascular ischemia secondary to impaired coronary flow reserve in the setting of diffuse atherosclerosis.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male with risk factors for atherosclerosis (hypertension, hyperlipidemia, diabetes, smoking).
- **Chief Complaint:** Severe, crushing chest pain at rest (while sleeping), without exertion or stress. This pattern is highly suggestive of vasospastic angina (Prinzmetal angina).
- **ECG Findings:** Transient ST-segment elevation in V1-V4, resolving spontaneously. This indicates acute myocardial ischemia.
- **Cardiac Biomarkers:** Normal troponin levels rule out significant myocardial infarction (necrosis).
- **Coronary Angiogram:** No significant fixed stenoses, but diffuse non-obstructive disease. This rules out typical stable angina caused by fixed blockages.
- **Provocative Testing:** Intracoronary acetylcholine administration induces spasm in the LAD, correlating with pain and ECG changes. Acetylcholine is a potent stimulus for endothelial nitric oxide (NO) release. In patients with vasospastic angina, endothelial dysfunction leads to impaired NO production, resulting in unopposed vasoconstriction mediated by alpha-adrenergic receptors and other contractile agonists.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely mechanism responsible for the recurrent chest pain.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- The clinical presentation (chest pain at rest, transient ST elevation, normal troponins) points towards vasospastic angina.
- The coronary angiogram showing no significant fixed stenosis and the positive provocative test with acetylcholine confirm the diagnosis.
- Acetylcholine normally stimulates endothelial cells to release NO, a potent vasodilator. In vasospastic angina, endothelial dysfunction impairs NO release.
- Without sufficient NO-mediated vasodilation, vasoconstrictive stimuli (like acetylcholine itself, or endogenous catecholamines) cause excessive smooth muscle contraction in the coronary arteries.
- This excessive contraction leads to transient, severe luminal narrowing (spasm), reducing blood flow and causing myocardial ischemia, manifesting as chest pain and ST elevation.
- Therefore, the underlying mechanism is transient endothelial dysfunction leading to excessive coronary artery smooth muscle contraction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Rupture of an atherosclerotic plaque leading to thrombus formation and coronary occlusion. This describes the mechanism of acute myocardial infarction (MI) or unstable angina. While the patient has risk factors for atherosclerosis, the angiogram showed no significant fixed stenosis, the troponins were normal, and the pain was episodic and provoked by acetylcholine, not a thrombotic event.
- (B) Increased myocardial oxygen demand due to elevated heart rate and contractility during exertion. This is the classic mechanism for stable angina pectoris, typically triggered by physical activity or emotional stress. The patient's pain occurred at rest, and the angiogram showed no obstructive disease limiting supply.
- (D) Progressive intimal hyperplasia and fibrosis causing fixed luminal narrowing of the coronary arteries. This describes the pathophysiology of chronic stable angina due to atherosclerosis. The angiogram specifically ruled out significant fixed stenoses.
- (E) Microvascular ischemia secondary to impaired coronary flow reserve in the setting of diffuse atherosclerosis. While microvascular dysfunction can cause angina-like symptoms, the hallmark of this patient's condition is macroscopic coronary artery spasm, demonstrated by the provocative test. Microvascular ischemia typically presents with more diffuse symptoms and may not show the characteristic ST elevation seen here, and the provocative test would target the larger epicardial vessels.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Vasospastic Angina (Prinzmetal) | Stable Angina (Fixed Stenosis) | Acute MI (Plaque Rupture) | Microvascular Angina |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------------|----------------------------|-----------------------|
| **Timing of Pain**      | Often at rest, night/early morning | Typically with exertion        | Sudden, severe, prolonged | Variable, often exertion |
| **ECG**                 | Transient ST elevation          | ST depression (usually)        | Persistent ST elevation   | Often normal or non-specific |
| **Troponins**           | Normal                          | Normal (usually)                | Elevated                   | Normal                |
| **Angiogram**           | No fixed stenosis, spasm provoked | Fixed stenosis (>70%)          | Occlusion, thrombus       | Normal epicardial arteries |
| **Mechanism**           | Coronary artery spasm           | Supply-demand mismatch         | Thrombosis                | Microvascular dysfunction |
| **Provocative Test**    | Positive (e.g., acetylcholine)  | Negative                       | Not applicable            | Not applicable        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Vasospastic angina (Prinzmetal angina) is characterized by episodic chest pain, often at rest, due to transient coronary artery spasm. It is caused by endothelial dysfunction leading to impaired vasodilation and excessive smooth muscle contraction. Key diagnostic features include transient ST elevation on ECG, normal cardiac biomarkers, and the absence of significant fixed coronary stenosis on angiography, often confirmed by provocative testing. Differentiate it from stable angina (fixed stenosis, exertion-related), acute MI (plaque rupture, thrombosis, elevated troponins), and microvascular angina (normal epicardial arteries, microvascular dysfunction).

<a id='question-029'></a>

### Question 029: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 01-29 ((vasodilatation).Electrocardiogramshows transient )
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and histopathological features of Takotsubo cardiomyopathy (stress-induced cardiomyopathy), differentiating it from acute myocardial infarction, and recognizing the characteristic ECG changes and cardiac imaging findings. (理解章鱼烧心肌病的病理生理学和组织病理学特征，将其与急性心肌梗塞区分开来，并识别其特征性心电图和心脏影像学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old woman presents to the emergency department complaining of sudden onset, severe chest pain and shortness of breath that began approximately 2 hours ago after receiving distressing news about her son's serious illness. Her vital signs show a blood pressure of 110/70 mmHg, heart rate of 110 bpm, respiratory rate of 24 breaths/min, and oxygen saturation of 92% on room air. Physical examination reveals diffuse ST-segment elevation on her electrocardiogram (ECG), mimicking an acute myocardial infarction. Cardiac troponin levels are elevated (0.8 ng/mL, reference range <0.04 ng/mL), but serial ECGs show transient T-wave inversions and QT prolongation. Echocardiography reveals apical ballooning (akinesis) with hyperkinesis of the basal segments of the left ventricle, consistent with a left ventricular ejection fraction (LVEF) of 35%. Coronary angiography shows no significant obstructive coronary artery disease.

What is the most likely underlying pathophysiological mechanism responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Rupture of atherosclerotic plaque leading to coronary artery thrombosis and subsequent myocardial necrosis.
(B) Catecholamine-induced myocardial stunning and microvascular dysfunction.
(C) Autoimmune attack against cardiac myocytes, causing myocarditis and ventricular dysfunction.
(D) Ischemic injury secondary to severe aortic stenosis causing demand ischemia.
(E) Genetic mutation in the cardiac troponin T gene leading to hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old woman.
- **Trigger:** Sudden onset of symptoms after significant emotional stress (distressing news).
- **Symptoms:** Severe chest pain, shortness of breath.
- **Vital Signs:** Tachycardia (110 bpm), tachypnea (24 breaths/min), mild hypoxia (SpO2 92%).
- **ECG:** Diffuse ST-segment elevation (mimics STEMI), transient T-wave inversions, QT prolongation.
- **Cardiac Biomarkers:** Elevated troponin (0.8 ng/mL), indicating myocardial injury.
- **Echocardiography:** Apical ballooning (akinesis) with basal hyperkinesis, reduced LVEF (35%).
- **Coronary Angiography:** No significant obstructive coronary artery disease.
- **Question:** Underlying pathophysiological mechanism.

The combination of acute chest pain, ECG changes mimicking STEMI, elevated troponin, characteristic echocardiographic findings (apical ballooning), absence of obstructive coronary artery disease, and a clear emotional trigger strongly points towards Takotsubo cardiomyopathy (stress-induced cardiomyopathy). The key mechanism involves excessive catecholamine release triggered by stress, leading to myocardial stunning and microvascular dysfunction, particularly affecting the apical region.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
Takotsubo cardiomyopathy (TCM) is characterized by transient left ventricular dysfunction, typically apical ballooning, often precipitated by intense emotional or physical stress. The proposed pathophysiology involves a surge in catecholamines (epinephrine, norepinephrine) released in response to the stressor. These catecholamines bind to β-adrenergic receptors on cardiomyocytes and vascular smooth muscle cells.
- **Myocardial Effects:** Excessive catecholamine stimulation can lead to:
    - Direct cardiotoxicity: Catecholamines can cause calcium overload in cardiomyocytes, leading to mitochondrial dysfunction, oxidative stress, and impaired contractility (myocardial stunning).
    - Microvascular dysfunction: Catecholamines can cause coronary vasospasm or microvascular spasm, reducing blood flow to the myocardium, particularly the apex which has a higher density of β2-adrenergic receptors.
    - Myocyte apoptosis/necrosis: Prolonged or severe catecholamine exposure can induce apoptosis or necrosis.
- **ECG Changes:** The ECG typically shows ST-segment elevation (mimicking STEMI), often diffuse, followed by T-wave inversions and QT prolongation. These changes reflect the transient myocardial dysfunction and repolarization abnormalities.
- **Histopathology:** While often normal or showing non-specific changes, some studies have shown evidence of myocardial edema, hemorrhage, and scattered myocyte necrosis, particularly in the apical region. Immunohistochemistry may show increased catecholamine staining.
- **Clinical Course:** The condition is typically self-limiting, with ventricular function recovering within days to weeks.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Rupture of atherosclerotic plaque leading to coronary artery thrombosis and subsequent myocardial necrosis.** This describes the pathophysiology of an acute ST-elevation myocardial infarction (STEMI). While the patient presents with chest pain, ECG changes, and elevated troponin, the absence of obstructive coronary artery disease on angiography and the characteristic apical ballooning on echocardiography rule out STEMI as the primary diagnosis. The ECG changes in STEMI are typically localized to specific coronary artery territories, not diffuse, and the echocardiogram would show regional wall motion abnormalities corresponding to the infarct territory, not apical ballooning.
- (C): **Autoimmune attack against cardiac myocytes, causing myocarditis and ventricular dysfunction.** This describes myocarditis. Myocarditis can present with chest pain, ECG changes, and elevated troponin. However, the typical trigger is often viral or infectious, although autoimmune causes exist. Echocardiography in myocarditis usually shows global hypokinesis or regional wall motion abnormalities, not the specific apical ballooning pattern seen in Takotsubo cardiomyopathy. Histopathology would show lymphocytic infiltration.
- (D): **Ischemic injury secondary to severe aortic stenosis causing demand ischemia.** Severe aortic stenosis can cause chest pain (angina) due to increased myocardial oxygen demand and reduced supply. While it can lead to ventricular dysfunction and elevated troponin, the ECG changes are typically non-specific (e.g., LVH, T-wave inversions), not diffuse ST-elevation. Echocardiography would reveal the underlying aortic stenosis and typically concentric left ventricular hypertrophy, not apical ballooning. Coronary angiography would be normal unless there is coexisting coronary artery disease.
- (E): **Genetic mutation in the cardiac troponin T gene leading to hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.** Mutations in cardiac troponin genes (TNNT2, TNNI3, TNNC1) are associated with hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM), not Takotsubo cardiomyopathy. HCM is characterized by left ventricular hypertrophy, often asymmetric, and diastolic dysfunction. While chest pain and ECG changes can occur in HCM, the presentation described (acute onset after stress, apical ballooning, elevated troponin without obstructive CAD) is not typical for HCM.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Takotsubo Cardiomyopathy (Stress-Induced) | Acute Myocardial Infarction (STEMI) | Myocarditis                  | Severe Aortic Stenosis | Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :----------------------- | :--------------------------- |
| **Trigger**             | Emotional/Physical Stress                 | Atherosclerotic Plaque Rupture     | Viral/Autoimmune Infection  | Increased Myocardial O2 Demand | Genetic Mutation             |
| **ECG**                 | Diffuse ST-elevation, TWI, QT prolongation | Localized ST-elevation, Q waves    | Variable (ST/T changes)      | LVH, Non-specific changes | LVH, TWI, ST depression      |
| **Troponin**            | Elevated                                  | Elevated                           | Elevated                     | Elevated                 | Normal/Slightly Elevated     |
| **Coronary Angiography** | Normal/Non-obstructive                    | Obstructive CAD                    | Normal                       | Normal                   | Normal                       |
| **Echocardiography**    | Apical Ballooning, Basal Hyperkinesis      | Regional Wall Motion Abnormalities | Global/Regional Hypokinesis | LVH, Diastolic Dysfunction | LVH, Diastolic Dysfunction |
| **Pathophysiology**     | Catecholamine-induced Stunning            | Thrombosis, Myocardial Necrosis    | Inflammation, Myocyte Damage | Demand Ischemia          | Myocyte Hypertrophy          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Takotsubo cardiomyopathy is a stress-induced cardiomyopathy characterized by transient left ventricular dysfunction, often apical ballooning, in the absence of obstructive coronary artery disease. It is triggered by catecholamine surge. Key differentials include acute myocardial infarction (STEMI), myocarditis, and other causes of chest pain and elevated troponin. Recognizing the specific clinical context (stress trigger), ECG pattern (diffuse ST elevation), and echocardiographic findings (apical ballooning) is crucial for diagnosis.

<a id='question-030'></a>

### Question 030: Cardiac Pathology: Transmural Ischemia
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the histopathological changes associated with transmural myocardial infarction (MI) and differentiating it from other causes of myocardial injury or ischemia. (理解跨心肌梗死 (MI) 的组织病理学变化，并将其与其他导致心肌损伤或缺血的原因区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing chest pain radiating to his left arm, which started 3 hours ago. He has a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia. On examination, his blood pressure is 90/60 mmHg, heart rate is 110 bpm, and respiratory rate is 24 breaths/min. An ECG shows ST-segment elevation in leads II, III, and aVF. Cardiac troponin I levels are markedly elevated (reference range <0.04 ng/mL). An echocardiogram reveals severe global hypokinesis. The patient undergoes emergent coronary angiography, which reveals complete occlusion of the right coronary artery (RCA). Three days later, the patient dies due to cardiogenic shock. Autopsy is performed, and a section of the left ventricle is examined histologically. The microscopic examination reveals loss of myocardial cells throughout the full thickness of the ventricular wall, replaced by amorphous granular debris and neutrophils. The remaining viable myocytes show signs of coagulative necrosis at the periphery of the infarct zone. There is evidence of significant interstitial edema and hemorrhage.

Which of the following histopathological findings is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Wavy fibers (Ondine fibers)
(B) Contraction band necrosis
(C) Granulomatous inflammation with giant cells
(D) Full-thickness necrosis with transmural loss of myocytes and inflammatory infiltrate
(E) Myocyte hypertrophy with interstitial fibrosis

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia).
- **Presentation:** Acute onset of severe crushing chest pain radiating to the left arm (classic angina pectoris).
- **Vital Signs:** Hypotension (90/60 mmHg), tachycardia (110 bpm), tachypnea (24 breaths/min) - suggestive of cardiogenic shock.
- **ECG:** ST-segment elevation in inferior leads (II, III, aVF) - indicates acute inferior wall myocardial infarction.
- **Labs:** Markedly elevated cardiac troponin I - confirms myocardial necrosis.
- **Echocardiogram:** Severe global hypokinesis - indicates significant myocardial dysfunction.
- **Angiography:** Complete occlusion of the RCA - confirms the cause of the inferior wall MI.
- **Outcome:** Death due to cardiogenic shock after 3 days.
- **Autopsy Findings:** Histology shows loss of myocardial cells throughout the full thickness of the ventricular wall, replaced by debris and neutrophils. Coagulative necrosis at the periphery. Interstitial edema and hemorrhage.
- **Question:** Asks for the most characteristic histopathological finding.

The clinical presentation, ECG, elevated troponin, angiography findings, and autopsy description are all consistent with an acute transmural myocardial infarction (MI) affecting the inferior wall of the left ventricle due to RCA occlusion. The key histopathological feature described is necrosis extending through the *full thickness* of the myocardial wall (transmural) with associated inflammation (neutrophils).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Mechanism of MI:** Prolonged ischemia (due to coronary artery occlusion) leads to ATP depletion, failure of ion pumps (Na+/K+ ATPase, Ca2+ ATPase), influx of Na+ and Ca2+, cellular swelling, mitochondrial damage, and ultimately irreversible cell injury (coagulative necrosis).
- **Transmural MI:** Ischemia affects the entire thickness of the myocardial wall, from the endocardium to the epicardium. This is typically caused by occlusion of a major coronary artery (like the RCA in this case).
- **Histopathology of MI:**
    - **Early (0-12 hours):** Minimal changes, maybe wavy fibers (Ondine fibers) at the periphery.
    - **Early Necrosis (12-24 hours):** Coagulative necrosis, edema, hemorrhage, early neutrophil infiltration.
    - **Established Necrosis (1-3 days):** Coagulative necrosis is well-established, maximal neutrophil infiltration (peak at 1-3 days), fragmentation of necrotic myocytes, beginning of phagocytosis of dead cells by neutrophils. The necrosis extends through the full thickness in a transmural MI.
    - **Later Stages (days to weeks):** Macrophage infiltration, granulation tissue formation, collagen deposition, scar formation.
- **Key Feature in this Case:** The description explicitly states "loss of myocardial cells throughout the full thickness of the ventricular wall," which defines transmural necrosis. The presence of neutrophils and debris confirms the necrotic process is underway (consistent with the 3-day post-infarction timeline).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Wavy fibers (Ondine fibers):** These are hypercontracted myocytes seen at the *edge* of an early (0-12 hours) myocardial infarct, representing sublethal ischemic injury. They are not characteristic of established transmural necrosis seen 3 days later, which involves full-thickness cell death. This option describes an early, non-lethal change.
- (B) **Contraction band necrosis:** This type of necrosis is characterized by hypercontraction of myofibrils, forming dense bands. It is typically seen in conditions of severe, prolonged ischemia followed by reperfusion (e.g., after thrombolysis or angioplasty) or in conditions causing hypermetabolic states like catecholamine excess or heat stroke. While it can occur in MI, it's not the *defining* feature of a transmural MI described here, which primarily shows coagulative necrosis. The vignette describes a complete occlusion without mention of reperfusion.
- (C) **Granulomatous inflammation with giant cells:** This pattern is characteristic of specific infections (e.g., tuberculosis, fungal infections) or foreign body reactions. It is not a feature of myocardial infarction. Myocardial inflammation in MI is primarily neutrophilic early on, followed by macrophages.
- (E) **Myocyte hypertrophy with interstitial fibrosis:** This describes chronic changes seen in conditions like chronic hypertension or hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, representing adaptation to increased workload or underlying disease processes. It is not characteristic of acute myocardial infarction, which involves cell death (necrosis), not hypertrophy.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Transmural MI (Correct Answer)                                  | Wavy Fibers (A)                               | Contraction Band Necrosis (B)                     | Granulomatous Inflammation (C)                 | Myocyte Hypertrophy/Fibrosis (E)               |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Timing**              | Established (1-3 days)                                          | Early (0-12 hours)                              | Often post-reperfusion, severe ischemia           | N/A (Infectious/Foreign Body)                  | Chronic                                        |
| **Location**            | Full thickness of myocardium                                    | Periphery of infarct zone                        | Can occur in MI, but not defining feature        | N/A                                            | Diffuse or focal, chronic                      |
| **Cellular Change**     | Coagulative necrosis, full-thickness loss, inflammation (PMNs) | Hypercontraction of myocytes (sublethal injury) | Hypercontraction of myofibrils (often reperfusion) | Giant cells, granulomas                         | Enlarged myocytes, collagen deposition         |
| **Key Histology**       | Full-thickness necrosis, neutrophils, debris                     | Wavy myocytes                                  | Dense bands within myocytes                       | Granulomas, giant cells                         | Thickened walls, fibrosis                      |
| **Clinical Context**    | Acute MI with complete coronary occlusion                       | Early phase of MI                               | MI with reperfusion, catecholamine excess        | Infection, foreign body reaction                | Chronic heart disease (HTN, HCM)               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The hallmark of a transmural myocardial infarction is coagulative necrosis extending through the full thickness of the ventricular wall, accompanied by an acute inflammatory response (neutrophils). Differentiating this from early changes (wavy fibers), reperfusion injury (contraction band necrosis), non-ischemic causes (granulomatous inflammation), or chronic changes (hypertrophy/fibrosis) is crucial. Remember that transmural infarcts typically result from occlusion of major coronary arteries.

<a id='question-031'></a>

### Question 031: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 01-31 (KP00710.indb 105)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and histopathological features of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM), specifically focusing on the role of sarcomeric protein mutations leading to myofibrillar disarray and diastolic dysfunction. (理解肥厚型心肌病的病理生理学和组织病理学特征，特别是肌节蛋白突变导致肌纤维紊乱和舒张功能障碍的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male athlete presents to the emergency department complaining of exertional dyspnea and near-syncope during a recent basketball game. He has no significant past medical history and denies chest pain or palpitations. His family history is notable for sudden cardiac death in his uncle at age 40 during exercise. Physical examination reveals a harsh systolic murmur heard best at the left lower sternal border, which increases with Valsalva maneuver. His vital signs are stable: BP 125/78 mmHg, HR 72 bpm, RR 16/min, SpO2 98% on room air. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH) and T-wave inversions in the lateral leads. An echocardiogram reveals asymmetric septal hypertrophy (septal thickness 1.8 cm, free wall thickness 1.1 cm), systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve, and left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction with a peak gradient of 45 mmHg at rest. Cardiac MRI confirms the echocardiogram findings and shows extensive myocardial fibrosis, particularly in the basal septum, detected via late gadolinium enhancement (LGE). Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *MYH7* gene.

Which of the following pathological mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the observed left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction in this patient?

(A) Myocardial ischemia secondary to coronary artery compression by the hypertrophied septum.
(B) Increased contractility leading to systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve leaflet.
(C) Impaired relaxation and diastolic dysfunction due to increased myocardial stiffness.
(D) Ventricular septal defect (VSD) causing turbulent flow across the defect.
(E) Left atrial enlargement leading to mitral valve prolapse.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male athlete with exertional symptoms (dyspnea, near-syncope) and family history of sudden cardiac death. This points towards a potential inherited cardiac condition, often presenting in young adults.
- **Physical Exam:** Harsh systolic murmur at the left lower sternal border, increasing with Valsalva. This is characteristic of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy (HOCM). Valsalva decreases preload, worsening the obstruction and murmur.
- **ECG:** LVH and T-wave inversions are common findings in HCM.
- **Echocardiogram:** Asymmetric septal hypertrophy (ASH), SAM of the mitral valve, and LVOT obstruction are hallmark features of HOCM. The gradient confirms the obstruction.
- **Cardiac MRI:** Confirms ASH and shows myocardial fibrosis (LGE), a common finding in HCM associated with increased risk of arrhythmias and sudden death.
- **Genetic Testing:** *MYH7* mutation confirms a diagnosis of HCM, as mutations in sarcomeric proteins (like beta-myosin heavy chain encoded by *MYH7*) are the most common cause.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism *most directly* responsible for LVOT obstruction.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **HCM Pathophysiology:** HCM is characterized by unexplained LVH, often asymmetric (ASH), predominantly affecting the septum. This hypertrophy is due to mutations in genes encoding sarcomeric proteins (e.g., *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*), leading to abnormal myofibril assembly, increased stiffness, diastolic dysfunction, and myofibrillar disarray.
- **LVOT Obstruction Mechanism:** The primary mechanism of LVOT obstruction in HOCM is the systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve leaflet. During systole, the hypertrophied septum bulges into the LVOT. Simultaneously, the anterior mitral valve leaflet is pulled towards the septum due to the Venturi effect created by the high-velocity flow through the narrowed outflow tract and altered papillary muscle dynamics. This contact between the leaflet and the septum causes obstruction.
- **Role of Hypertrophy and Contractility:** The hypertrophy itself narrows the LVOT. Increased contractility (often seen in athletes or during stress) exacerbates the obstruction by increasing the velocity of blood flow through the narrowed space, intensifying the Venturi effect and promoting SAM.
- **Diastolic Dysfunction:** While diastolic dysfunction (impaired relaxation and filling) is a key feature of HCM due to increased stiffness, it does not directly cause the *systolic* LVOT obstruction.
- **Myocardial Fibrosis:** Fibrosis contributes to stiffness and diastolic dysfunction and increases the risk of arrhythmias but is not the primary cause of LVOT obstruction.
- **Coronary Compression:** While severe hypertrophy can potentially compress small coronary arteries, ischemia is not the primary mechanism of LVOT obstruction.
- **VSD:** A VSD is a separate congenital defect and not the cause of obstruction in HCM.
- **Mitral Valve Prolapse:** Mitral valve prolapse is a different condition involving leaflet redundancy and posterior displacement, not typically associated with SAM in HCM.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Myocardial ischemia secondary to coronary artery compression *can* occur in severe HCM, but it is not the primary mechanism causing the LVOT obstruction described (SAM). The obstruction is a direct consequence of the hypertrophied septum and mitral valve dynamics during systole.
- (C): Impaired relaxation and diastolic dysfunction are major features of HCM due to increased myocardial stiffness and myofibrillar disarray. However, this relates to filling problems, not the *systolic* obstruction of the outflow tract. While diastolic dysfunction contributes to symptoms, it doesn't directly cause the LVOT obstruction.
- (D): A ventricular septal defect (VSD) is a hole between the ventricles. It causes a murmur and can lead to volume overload, but it does not cause the specific type of obstruction (LVOT obstruction due to SAM) seen in HCM. HCM involves hypertrophy, not a septal hole.
- (E): Left atrial enlargement is a consequence of diastolic dysfunction and increased left ventricular filling pressures in HCM. Mitral valve prolapse is a separate condition. Neither is the direct cause of the systolic LVOT obstruction in HCM. The obstruction is caused by SAM of the mitral valve leaflet against the hypertrophied septum.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM) with LVOT Obstruction | Myocardial Ischemia | Diastolic Dysfunction | Ventricular Septal Defect (VSD) | Mitral Valve Prolapse |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|-----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------|
| **Primary Defect**   | Sarcomeric protein mutation -> Myofibrillar disarray, Hypertrophy | Atherosclerosis/Thrombosis -> Reduced blood flow | Impaired relaxation/stiffness | Hole in interventricular septum | Mitral valve leaflet redundancy |
| **LVOT Obstruction** | Yes (due to SAM)                                      | No                   | No                    | No                              | No                    |
| **Murmur**           | Systolic, harsh, LLSB, ↑ with Valsalva                | Variable             | None                  | Holosystolic, harsh, LLSB        | Mid-systolic click, late systolic murmur |
| **ECG**              | LVH, T-wave inversions                               | ST depression/elevation, T-wave inversion | LVH (if underlying cause)       | LVH (if large)          | Variable             |
| **Echocardiogram**   | ASH, SAM, LVOT gradient                               | Regional wall motion abnormalities | Normal/Diastolic dysfunction | Defect visualization        | Leaflet prolapse       |
| **Key Mechanism**    | SAM of mitral valve leaflet                             | Ischemia/Infarction  | Impaired filling      | Turbulent flow across defect    | Leaflet prolapse       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM) with left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction is primarily caused by systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve leaflet against the hypertrophied interventricular septum during systole. Remember to differentiate HCM from other causes of LVH (e.g., hypertension, aortic stenosis) and other causes of murmurs or dyspnea. Key features include asymmetric septal hypertrophy, SAM, and a murmur that increases with maneuvers decreasing preload (Valsalva, standing).

<a id='question-032'></a>

### Question 032: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 01-32 (13/10/22 1:52P)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and histopathological features of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM), specifically focusing on the role of sarcomeric protein mutations and myocardial disarray (肌细胞增生性心肌病，病理生理机制，心肌细胞排列紊乱).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male athlete presents to the emergency department after experiencing syncope during a basketball game. He reports occasional episodes of exertional dyspnea and palpitations over the past year. His family history is significant for sudden cardiac death in his father at age 40 during a soccer match. On examination, his blood pressure is 120/80 mmHg, heart rate is 75 bpm, and respiratory rate is 16 breaths/min. Cardiac auscultation reveals a harsh systolic murmur that increases with Valsalva maneuver. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH) and T-wave inversions. An echocardiogram demonstrates asymmetric septal hypertrophy (septal thickness 1.8 cm, free wall thickness 1.1 cm), left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction, and systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve. Cardiac magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) reveals patchy myocardial fibrosis, predominantly in the basal septum. Endomyocardial biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination shows marked myocyte hypertrophy, interstitial fibrosis, and significant disarray of the myocardial fibers, particularly in the interventricular septum. Immunohistochemical staining for cardiac troponin T is positive.

Which of the following pathological findings is the MOST characteristic histopathological hallmark of this patient's condition?

(A) Granulomatous inflammation with multinucleated giant cells and non-caseating granulomas.
(B) Replacement of viable myocardium with sheets of monotonous, pleomorphic cells with vesicular nuclei and prominent nucleoli.
(C) Myocyte hypertrophy, interstitial fibrosis, and myofiber disarray.
(D) Accumulation of eosinophilic, amorphous material within the myocardial cells and interstitium, staining positive with Congo red.
(E) Coagulative necrosis of myocardial fibers with neutrophil infiltration, typically seen in the subendocardium.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** Young male athlete with syncope, dyspnea, palpitations. Family history of sudden cardiac death. This points towards a potential inherited cardiac condition.
- **Physical Exam:** Harsh systolic murmur increasing with Valsalva suggests hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy (HOCM).
- **ECG:** LVH and T-wave inversions are consistent with HCM.
- **Echocardiogram:** Asymmetric septal hypertrophy, LVOT obstruction, and SAM are classic findings of HOCM.
- **Cardiac MRI:** Patchy myocardial fibrosis, especially in the basal septum, is common in HCM and increases the risk of arrhythmias.
- **Endomyocardial Biopsy:** The question asks for the characteristic histopathological finding. The vignette describes marked myocyte hypertrophy, interstitial fibrosis, and significant disarray of myocardial fibers. This is the hallmark of HCM.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM):** This is a genetic disorder characterized by unexplained left ventricular hypertrophy, often asymmetric (especially septal), in the absence of other causes like hypertension or aortic stenosis.
- **Pathophysiology:** HCM is primarily caused by mutations in genes encoding sarcomeric proteins (e.g., β-myosin heavy chain, myosin-binding protein C, troponin T, troponin I). These mutations disrupt the normal structure and function of the sarcomere, leading to increased contractility, diastolic dysfunction, and myocardial fibrosis.
- **Histopathology:** The key histopathological features are:
    - **Myocyte hypertrophy:** Individual myocytes are significantly enlarged.
    - **Interstitial fibrosis:** Increased collagen deposition between myocytes.
    - **Myofiber disarray:** Chaotic arrangement of myocardial fibers, particularly prominent in the interventricular septum. This disarray disrupts normal electrical conduction and contributes to arrhythmias.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Symptoms include dyspnea, chest pain, palpitations, syncope, and sudden cardiac death (due to ventricular arrhythmias). The murmur is due to LVOT obstruction caused by septal hypertrophy and SAM of the mitral valve.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Granulomatous inflammation with multinucleated giant cells and non-caseating granulomas.** This describes cardiac sarcoidosis, an inflammatory condition where non-caseating granulomas infiltrate the myocardium, leading to conduction abnormalities, heart failure, and arrhythmias. It is not characteristic of HCM.
- (B): **Replacement of viable myocardium with sheets of monotonous, pleomorphic cells with vesicular nuclei and prominent nucleoli.** This describes cardiac infiltration by tumor cells, such as in primary cardiac lymphoma or metastatic carcinoma. It is not characteristic of HCM.
- (D): **Accumulation of eosinophilic, amorphous material within the myocardial cells and interstitium, staining positive with Congo red.** This describes cardiac amyloidosis, where amyloid protein deposits in the myocardium, leading to restrictive cardiomyopathy, heart failure, and conduction abnormalities. It is not characteristic of HCM.
- (E): **Coagulative necrosis of myocardial fibers with neutrophil infiltration, typically seen in the subendocardium.** This describes myocardial infarction (heart attack), where ischemia leads to cell death. While HCM patients can have myocardial infarction, this is not the primary or characteristic pathology of HCM itself.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM) | Cardiac Sarcoidosis | Cardiac Lymphoma/Metastasis | Cardiac Amyloidosis | Myocardial Infarction |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------------------ | :-------------------------- | :------------------ | :-------------------- |
| **Primary Pathology**   | Myocyte hypertrophy, fibrosis, disarray | Granulomas         | Tumor infiltration          | Amyloid deposition  | Coagulative necrosis  |
| **Histology**           | Hypertrophied myocytes, fibrosis, disarray | Non-caseating granulomas | Monotonous tumor cells    | Congo red (+) deposits | Neutrophils, necrosis |
| **Typical Location**    | Septum (often asymmetric)         | Patchy              | Diffuse/Focal               | Diffuse             | Subendocardium        |
| **Key Clinical Features** | LVH, LVOT obstruction, syncope, SCD | Arrhythmias, HF     | Variable, often late        | Restrictive HF      | Chest pain, ECG changes |
| **Echocardiogram**      | Asymmetric septal hypertrophy, SAM | Variable wall thickness | Variable                   | Thickened walls     | Regional wall motion abnormality |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is characterized by myocyte hypertrophy, interstitial fibrosis, and myofiber disarray, often due to sarcomeric gene mutations. Differentiating it from other causes of LVH (like hypertension) and other cardiomyopathies (like amyloidosis, sarcoidosis, infiltrative diseases) requires careful clinical evaluation, imaging, and sometimes biopsy. Remember the classic triad of histology: hypertrophy, fibrosis, and disarray.

<a id='question-033'></a>

### Question 033: Cardiac Pathology: Focus 01-33 (Physiology)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM), specifically the role of myosin heavy chain mutations leading to diastolic dysfunction and outflow tract obstruction. (理解肥厚型心肌病的病理生理学，特别是肌动蛋白重链突变导致舒张功能障碍和流出道梗阻的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male athlete presents to the emergency department complaining of exertional dyspnea, chest pain, and near-syncope during a recent basketball game. He has no significant past medical history and denies illicit drug use. His family history is notable for sudden cardiac death in his father at age 40 during exercise. On physical examination, his blood pressure is 120/80 mmHg, heart rate is 72 bpm, and respiratory rate is 18 breaths/min. Cardiac auscultation reveals a harsh systolic murmur that increases with Valsalva maneuver and decreases with squatting. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows left ventricular hypertrophy (LVH) and T-wave inversions. An echocardiogram demonstrates asymmetric septal hypertrophy (septal thickness 1.8 cm, free wall thickness 1.2 cm), systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve, and left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction with a peak gradient of 50 mmHg at rest. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *MYH7* gene.

Which of the following pathophysiological mechanisms BEST explains the exertional symptoms and LVOT obstruction observed in this patient?

(A) Increased myocardial contractility leading to excessive shortening of the papillary muscles and mitral valve prolapse.
(B) Impaired diastolic relaxation due to increased myocardial stiffness, resulting in elevated left ventricular end-diastolic pressure and pulmonary congestion.
(C) Disordered sarcomere assembly caused by the *MYH7* mutation, leading to hyperdynamic contraction, systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve, and LVOT obstruction.
(D) Decreased myocardial compliance secondary to chronic hypertension, resulting in concentric left ventricular hypertrophy and impaired filling.
(E) Reduced preload due to peripheral vasodilation, leading to decreased stroke volume and increased reliance on heart rate to maintain cardiac output.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male athlete with exertional symptoms (dyspnea, chest pain, near-syncope) and family history of sudden cardiac death. This points towards a potential inherited cardiac condition, often presenting in young adulthood.
- **Physical Exam:** Harsh systolic murmur increasing with Valsalva (decreases preload) and decreasing with squatting (increases preload/afterload). This dynamic murmur is characteristic of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy (HOCM).
- **ECG:** LVH and T-wave inversions are common findings in HCM.
- **Echocardiogram:** Asymmetric septal hypertrophy (ASH), SAM of the mitral valve, and LVOT obstruction are pathognomonic features of HCM. The gradient of 50 mmHg confirms significant obstruction.
- **Genetic Testing:** *MYH7* mutation confirms the diagnosis of HCM, as mutations in sarcomeric proteins (like beta-myosin heavy chain encoded by *MYH7*) are the most common cause.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the BEST explanation of exertional symptoms and LVOT obstruction.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **HCM Pathophysiology:** HCM is primarily caused by mutations in genes encoding sarcomeric proteins (e.g., *MYH7*, *MYBPC3*). These mutations lead to abnormal sarcomere structure and function.
- **Molecular Mechanism:** The *MYH7* mutation affects the beta-myosin heavy chain, a key component of the thick filament in the sarcomere. This leads to disordered sarcomere assembly, resulting in:
    - **Hypertrophy:** Primarily asymmetric septal hypertrophy, although other patterns exist.
    - **Diastolic Dysfunction:** Increased myocardial stiffness due to abnormal sarcomere function impairs relaxation and filling, leading to elevated left ventricular end-diastolic pressure (LVEDP). This is a major contributor to symptoms like dyspnea.
    - **Hyperdynamic Contraction:** The abnormal sarcomeres can lead to increased contractility and force generation.
    - **Systolic Anterior Motion (SAM):** During systole, the hyperdynamic anterior mitral valve leaflet is pulled towards the hypertrophied septum, obstructing the LVOT. This obstruction is dynamic and worsens with decreased preload (Valsalva) and improves with increased preload/afterload (squatting).
- **Exertional Symptoms:** Increased heart rate and contractility during exercise exacerbate the LVOT obstruction (due to increased force generation and faster contraction) and worsen diastolic dysfunction (less time for filling), leading to dyspnea, chest pain, and potentially syncope or sudden death.
- **Connecting Clues:** The patient's symptoms, physical exam findings (dynamic murmur), ECG, echocardiogram results (ASH, SAM, LVOT obstruction), and genetic mutation (*MYH7*) all point towards HCM. The question asks for the mechanism linking the genetic defect to the clinical presentation, specifically the LVOT obstruction. Option C directly addresses this by linking the *MYH7* mutation to disordered sarcomere assembly, hyperdynamic contraction, SAM, and subsequent LVOT obstruction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): While increased contractility is present in HCM, the primary mechanism of LVOT obstruction is SAM of the mitral valve due to the abnormal septum-leaflet interaction, not papillary muscle shortening or mitral valve prolapse. Papillary muscle dysfunction can occur but isn't the main driver of LVOT obstruction in HCM.
- (B): Impaired diastolic relaxation and increased LVEDP are significant features of HCM and contribute to symptoms like dyspnea. However, this option doesn't explain the LVOT obstruction, which is a key finding in this patient (murmur characteristics, echocardiogram). While diastolic dysfunction is crucial, it's not the *best* explanation for *both* exertional symptoms *and* LVOT obstruction.
- (D): Concentric LVH is typically seen in pressure overload conditions like chronic hypertension. HCM usually presents with asymmetric septal hypertrophy, not concentric hypertrophy. Furthermore, while decreased compliance occurs in HCM, it's due to the sarcomere defect, not primarily chronic hypertension (which isn't mentioned in the history).
- (E): Reduced preload can worsen LVOT obstruction in HCM (as seen with Valsalva), but it's not the underlying cause of the hypertrophy or the obstruction itself. The patient is an athlete, suggesting adequate preload, and the underlying issue is the structural and functional abnormality of the myocardium. Peripheral vasodilation is not a feature of HCM.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Hypertrophic Cardiomyopathy (HCM) | Dilated Cardiomyopathy (DCM) | Aortic Stenosis (AS) | Hypertensive Heart Disease |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|--------------------------------|-----------------------|-----------------------------|
| **LV Hypertrophy**  | Yes, often asymmetric septal      | No, chamber dilation          | Yes, concentric       | Yes, concentric             |
| **Diastolic Function**| Impaired (stiff ventricle)        | Impaired (dilated ventricle)  | Normal initially, then impaired | Impaired (stiff ventricle) |
| **Systolic Function**| Often normal or hyperdynamic      | Impaired                      | Normal initially, then impaired | Normal initially, then impaired |
| **LVOT Obstruction**| Yes, dynamic (SAM)                | No                            | Yes, fixed            | No                          |
| **Murmur**           | Systolic, harsh, increases w/ Valsalva | None or S3/S4                | Systolic, harsh, radiates | None                        |
| **Genetics**         | Yes (sarcomeric mutations)        | Yes (various)                 | No                   | No                          |
| **Key Mechanism**    | Disordered sarcomere assembly     | Myocyte injury/apoptosis      | Valve calcification   | Pressure overload           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (HCM) is characterized by asymmetric septal hypertrophy, diastolic dysfunction, and often dynamic left ventricular outflow tract (LVOT) obstruction due to systolic anterior motion (SAM) of the mitral valve. It is most commonly caused by mutations in genes encoding sarcomeric proteins, such as *MYH7*. Key differentials include aortic stenosis (fixed obstruction, different murmur characteristics), dilated cardiomyopathy (chamber dilation, systolic dysfunction), and hypertensive heart disease (concentric hypertrophy, usually related to chronic hypertension). Remember the dynamic nature of the murmur in HCM (worsens with decreased preload).
